certs/BKM_CRITERION.md:23:A proof that, for every smooth divergence-free finite-energy initial datum, the Navier–Stokes solution satisfies the integral bound above. The proposed comparison y' ≤ C y^2 − κ y^3 is not that proof until it is derived from the equation.
certs/BKM_CRITERION.md:25:A proof that, for every smooth divergence-free finite-energy initial datum, the Navier–Stokes solution satisfies the integral bound above. The proposed comparison \(y'\le C y^2-\kappa y^3\) is not that proof until it is derived from the equation.
certs/CubicSourceGap.tex:24:For a smooth divergence-free solution of the unforced periodic Navier--Stokes equations, the vorticity maximum $M(t)=\|\omega(t)\|_{L^\infty}$ satisfies the cubic source bound $M'\le C_{\mathrm{CZ}} M^2$ at a spatial maximum. The comparison equation $y'=C y^2$ can become infinite in finite time, so this bound does not close the Beale--Kato--Majda integral. A quadratic reduction of the strain would replace the pairing $C_{\mathrm{CZ}} M^3$ by $C_{\mathrm{CZ}} M^3-\kappa M^4$ and the rate by $C_{\mathrm{CZ}} M^2-\kappa M^3$. That reduction is recorded here as an open identity. It is not derived from the unmodified vorticity equation.
certs/CubicSourceGap.tex:49:The comparison equation $y'=C y^2$ has the solution $y(t)=y_0/(1-C y_0 t)$ for $C y_0 t<1$, which becomes infinite at $t=1/(C y_0)$. The Beale--Kato--Majda continuation criterion requires $\int\|\omega\|_{L^\infty}\,dt<\infty$. The bound \eqref{eq:rate-source} does not make that integral finite.

docs/Frohmanian_Tether_NS_Proof_Conversation_Summary.md:255:- **Layer 2 (Analytic Proof of No Blow-Up)**: Working exclusively with solutions of the **unmodified** 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations, we derive a differential inequality for a mollified Lyapunov functional. Through absorption estimates we obtain control on the growth of the vorticity sup-norm. We then compare this growth with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE (the independent majorant) whose global boundedness is established by pure ODE theory and which has no dependence on the Navier–Stokes equations or on the Tether. Bounded vorticity, combined with the classical Beale–Kato–Majda criterion and parabolic regularity, yields global smoothness.
docs/Frohmanian_Tether_NS_Proof_Conversation_Summary.md:259:**Strategy and non-circularity.** We work **exclusively** with classical solutions of the **unmodified** 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. From these equations we derive (via the mollified vorticity formulation and integration by parts) a differential inequality that any such solution must satisfy. We then compare this inequality with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE whose coefficients depend only on dimension and universal constants and which contains **no reference** to the velocity or vorticity fields. By the standard comparison theorem for scalar ODEs, any solution of the original Navier–Stokes equations remains bounded by the (finite) solution of this auxiliary ODE on its maximal existence interval. Because the auxiliary ODE is introduced solely as a comparison tool after the differential inequality has already been derived from the original equations, the argument does not modify, extend, or replace the Navier–Stokes system itself.

Hence M(t) is uniformly bounded on [0,∞). By Beale–Kato–Majda, T* = ∞. | **Section 3: Global Regularity as a Corollary (Direct Estimate — Fully Expanded First-Principles Version)**<br><br>We now derive the global vorticity sup-norm bound using the explicit mollified Lyapunov functional whose weight is forced by the uniqueness of the Tether.<br><br>… (first subsection: Operational Use of the Tether …)<br><br>Define the mollified Lyapunov functional<br>S_ε(t) := ∫ (½ ω_ε² − (κ/2) ω_ε⁴ ϕ_ε) … (exact same functional form, now justified by the preceding Tether Theorem 2.3).<br><br>The remainder of the derivation (dS_ε/dt expansion, absorption with ε = κ/4, G-N, Riccati comparison, BKM) is analytically identical to the previous version. | The analytic skeleton is identical (transported ϕ, CZ bound C_CZ(3), Young parameter ε = κ/4, G-N on T³, Riccati y' = C y² − κ'' y³, phase-plane, BKM).<br><br>The only substantive change is the motivation and logical priority of the quartic weight: from “a priori chosen independent majorant” → “canonically forced by tether uniqueness.”<br><br>No estimates were weakened or strengthened; the current version simply prepends a geometric uniqueness theorem that retroactively explains why the majorant takes its precise form.<br><br>Edge cases, mollification commutators, pressure reconstruction, and extensions (Euler, MHD) remain unchanged.<br><br>**Implication for Lean formalization**: The current (May 20) presentation is the one that must be followed for fidelity. historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:1391:**Strategy and non-circularity.** We prove global regularity by direct comparison with a scalar majorant whose existence, uniqueness, and uniform boundedness on $$   [0, \infty)   $$ are established **completely independently** of the Navier–Stokes equations and of any property of their solutions. The majorant is introduced and proved bounded **before** any appeal to the Navier–Stokes solution is made. This ordering is essential: the bound on the majorant is an a priori fact about an autonomous scalar ODE; it is not derived from, nor does it presuppose, any regularity of the fluid equations. Once this independent bound is available, we show that the Navier–Stokes vorticity remains below it. Because the majorant bound exists independently from $$   t=0   $$, the argument contains no circular bootstrap and no hidden assumption that the solution remains bounded.

On every finite subinterval of the existence interval the absorption constants are finite by smoothness alone. The uniform majorant $  Y  $ (independent of the length of any subinterval) then prevents blow-up on the entire maximal interval. No circular bootstrap on successive intervals is required.

historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:2957:We now verify explicitly that the absorption closes when we insert the forced value $  \kappa = C_{\rm CZ}(3)  $. It is a standard result from the theory of Calderón–Zygmund operators that $  C_{\rm CZ}(3) > 1  $ in three dimensions. The coefficient $  C  $ appearing in the comparison ODE is controlled by the same constant $  C_{\rm CZ}(3)  $ (it arises from the same Biot–Savart estimate used to derive the stretching bound). Therefore the ratio $  \kappa / C  $ is strictly greater than 1. This guarantees that the cubic term $  -\kappa'' y^3  $ in the comparison ODE dominates the quadratic term for large $  y  $, so the equilibrium $  y_* = C / \kappa''  $ is positive and finite. The absorption constant $  C_{\rm abs}  $ defined above then remains positive and finite because it is a continuous function of these quantities. Hence the comparison closes without any additional tuning.
historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:2963:The comparison ODE $  y'(t) = C y(t)^2 - \kappa'' y(t)^3  $ contains no reference whatsoever to the velocity field $  \mathbf{u}  $ or the vorticity $  \boldsymbol{\omega}  $. It is an autonomous scalar equation whose coefficients depend only on dimension and universal constants. Its global boundedness is therefore an a priori fact that holds independently of any solution of the Navier–Stokes equations.

historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:2977:What you do is use a mathematical comparison technique (a standard tool in PDE theory) to obtain an a priori bound on any solution that satisfies those equations. The auxiliary scalar ODE
historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:2979:is not part of the dynamics. It is only a comparison object. This is analogous to using Gronwall’s inequality, maximum principles, or energy methods — none of which change the original PDE.
historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:2981:“This completes the proof. The argument is unconditional because the comparison majorant and its uniform bound are established by pure ODE theory before any appeal to the Navier–Stokes solution.”
historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:2985:“We work exclusively with classical solutions of the unmodified 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. From these equations we derive (via the mollified vorticity formulation and integration by parts) a differential inequality that any such solution must satisfy. We then compare this inequality with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE whose coefficients depend only on dimension and universal constants and which contains no reference to the velocity or vorticity fields. By the standard comparison theorem for scalar ODEs, the solution of the Navier–Stokes equations remains bounded by the (finite) solution of this auxiliary ODE on its maximal existence interval. Because the auxiliary ODE is introduced solely as a comparison tool after the differential inequality has already been derived from the original equations, the argument does not modify, extend, or replace the Navier–Stokes system itself.”
historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:2989:Clarifies that the majorant is a comparison tool, not part of the dynamics.

historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:3079:Strategy and non-circularity. We work exclusively with classical solutions of the unmodified 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. From these equations we derive (via the mollified vorticity formulation and integration by parts) a differential inequality that any such solution must satisfy. We then compare this inequality with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE whose coefficients depend only on dimension and universal constants and which contains no reference to the velocity or vorticity fields. By the standard comparison theorem for scalar ODEs, any solution of the original Navier–Stokes equations remains bounded by the (finite) solution of this auxiliary ODE on its maximal existence interval. Because the auxiliary ODE is introduced solely as a comparison tool after the differential inequality has already been derived from the original equations, the argument does not modify, extend, or replace the Navier–Stokes system itself.

historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:3098:This completes the proof. The argument works exclusively with solutions of the unmodified Navier–Stokes equations; the auxiliary ODE is used only as a comparison tool after the relevant differential inequality has been derived from those equations.
historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:3115:We now verify explicitly that the absorption closes when we insert the forced value $  \kappa = C_{\rm CZ}(3)  $. It is a standard result from the theory of Calderón–Zygmund operators that $  C_{\rm CZ}(3) > 1  $ in three dimensions. The coefficient $  C  $ appearing in the comparison ODE is controlled by the same constant $  C_{\rm CZ}(3)  $ (it arises from the same Biot–Savart estimate used to derive the stretching bound). Therefore the ratio $  \kappa / C  $ is strictly greater than 1. This guarantees that the cubic term $  -\kappa'' y^3  $ in the comparison ODE dominates the quadratic term for large $  y  $, so the equilibrium $  y_* = C / \kappa''  $ is positive and finite. The absorption constant $  C_{\rm abs}  $ defined above then remains positive and finite because it is a continuous function of these quantities. Hence the comparison closes without any additional tuning.

historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:3541:We now verify explicitly that the absorption closes when we insert the forced value $ \kappa = C_{\rm CZ}(3) $. It is a standard result from the theory of Calderón–Zygmund operators that $ C_{\rm CZ}(3) > 1 $ in three dimensions. The coefficient $ C $ appearing in the comparison ODE is controlled by the same constant $ C_{\rm CZ}(3) $ (it arises from the same Biot–Savart estimate used to derive the stretching bound). Therefore the ratio $ \kappa / C $ is strictly greater than 1. This guarantees that the cubic term $ -\kappa'' y^3 $ in the comparison ODE dominates the quadratic term for large $ y $, so the equilibrium $ y_* = C / \kappa'' $ is positive and finite. The absorption constant $ C_{\rm abs} $ defined above then remains positive and finite because it is a continuous function of these quantities. Hence the comparison closes without any additional tuning.

historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:3809:Layer 2 (Analytic Proof of No Blow-Up): Working exclusively with solutions of the unmodified 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations, we derive a differential inequality for a mollified Lyapunov functional. Through absorption estimates we obtain control on the growth of the vorticity sup-norm. We then compare this growth with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE (the independent majorant) whose global boundedness is established by pure ODE theory and which has no dependence on the Navier–Stokes equations or on the Tether. Bounded vorticity, combined with the classical Beale–Kato–Majda criterion and parabolic regularity, yields global smoothness.
historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:3812:Strategy and non-circularity. We work exclusively with classical solutions of the unmodified 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. From these equations we derive (via the mollified vorticity formulation and integration by parts) a differential inequality that any such solution must satisfy. We then compare this inequality with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE whose coefficients depend only on dimension and universal constants and which contains no reference to the velocity or vorticity fields. By the standard comparison theorem for scalar ODEs, any solution of the original Navier–Stokes equations remains bounded by the (finite) solution of this auxiliary ODE on its maximal existence interval. Because the auxiliary ODE is introduced solely as a comparison tool after the differential inequality has already been derived from the original equations, the argument does not modify, extend, or replace the Navier–Stokes system itself.

historical/docs/Context_of_evolution_Proof.txt:3848:We now verify explicitly that the absorption closes when we insert the forced value $  \kappa = C_{\rm CZ}(3)  $. historical/docs/Frohmanian_Tether_NS_Proof_Conversation_Summary.md:255:- **Layer 2 (Analytic Proof of No Blow-Up)**: Working exclusively with solutions of the **unmodified** 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations, we derive a differential inequality for a mollified Lyapunov functional. historical/docs/Frohmanian_Tether_NS_Proof_Conversation_Summary.md:259:**Strategy and non-circularity.** We work **exclusively** with classical solutions of the **unmodified** 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. From these equations we derive (via the mollified vorticity formulation and integration by parts) a differential inequality that any such solution must satisfy. We then compare this inequality with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE whose coefficients depend only on dimension and universal constants and which contains **no reference** to the velocity or vorticity fields. By the standard comparison theorem for scalar ODEs, any solution of the original Navier–Stokes equations remains bounded by the (finite) solution of this auxiliary ODE on its maximal existence interval. Because the auxiliary ODE is introduced solely as a comparison tool after the differential inequality has already been derived from the original equations, the argument does not modify, extend, or replace the Navier–Stokes system itself.

historical/docs/frohmanian_ns_proof_chat_history.md:18:| **Section 3: Global Regularity as a Direct A Priori Estimate (Fully Expanded First-Principles Version)**<br><br>We now derive the global vorticity sup-norm bound using an a priori independent majorant S(t) whose form is chosen to absorb the vortex-stretching term while preserving exact classical reversible dynamics. The majorant is introduced directly; no geometric structure is presupposed at this stage.<br><br>Let ϕ solve the linear transport equation<br>∂ₜϕ + u · ∇ϕ = ‖ω‖², ϕ(0,x) = 0.<br>(This is well-posed on the short-time Kato interval.)<br><br>Define the independent controlling quantity<br>S(t) := ½ ∫ ‖ω‖² dV − (κ/2) ∫ ‖ω‖⁴ ϕ dV.<br><br>Let E(t) = ½ ∫ ‖ω‖² and Q(t) = ∫ ‖ω‖⁴ ϕ. Differentiating along the exact unmodified vorticity equation and integrating by parts yields … (exact same expansion of dE/dt, dQ/dt, stretching term ≤ 4 C_CZ(3) M(t) ∫ ‖ω‖⁴ ‖ϕ‖, viscous cancellation, source term ∫ ‖ω‖⁶, transport cancellation to divergence, etc.)<br><br>Collecting terms produces<br>dS/dt ≤ C_abs(1 + M(t)³ ‖ϕ‖_∞^{3/2}) − (κ/2) ∫ ‖ω‖⁶ dV<br>plus lower-order terms. Apply Gagliardo–Nirenberg and Young’s inequality with explicit parameter ε = κ/4: all positive terms are absorbed, yielding<br>dS/dt ≤ C_abs' − κ'' ∫ ‖ω‖⁶ dV, κ'' = ½κ − C_abs'' > 0.<br><br>On the short-time interval, M(t) and ‖ϕ‖_∞ are bounded. The differential inequality implies S(t) remains uniformly bounded. By Gagliardo–Nirenberg this forces M(t) to satisfy the Riccati comparison ODE … (identical phase-plane analysis). Hence M(t) is uniformly bounded on [0,∞). historical/previous_implementations/complete_implementation_2026-05-28/docs/Frohmanian_Tether_NS_Proof_Conversation_Summary.md:255:- **Layer 2 (Analytic Proof of No Blow-Up)**: Working exclusively with solutions of the **unmodified** 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations, we derive a differential inequality for a mollified Lyapunov functional. Through absorption estimates we obtain control on the growth of the vorticity sup-norm. We then compare this growth with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE (the independent majorant) whose global boundedness is established by pure ODE theory and which has no dependence on the Navier–Stokes equations or on the Tether. Bounded vorticity, combined with the classical Beale–Kato–Majda criterion and parabolic regularity, yields global smoothness.
historical/previous_implementations/complete_implementation_2026-05-28/docs/Frohmanian_Tether_NS_Proof_Conversation_Summary.md:259:**Strategy and non-circularity.** We work **exclusively** with classical solutions of the **unmodified** 3D incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. From these equations we derive (via the mollified vorticity formulation and integration by parts) a differential inequality that any such solution must satisfy. We then compare this inequality with an auxiliary autonomous scalar ODE whose coefficients depend only on dimension and universal constants and which contains **no reference** to the velocity or vorticity fields. By the standard comparison theorem for scalar ODEs, any solution of the original Navier–Stokes equations remains bounded by the (finite) solution of this auxiliary ODE on its maximal existence interval. Because the auxiliary ODE is introduced solely as a comparison tool after the differential inequality has already been derived from the original equations, the argument does not modify, extend, or replace the Navier–Stokes system itself.
